            <div class="main-content lit-style" itemscope="itemscope" itemtype="http://schema.org/CreativeWork"><div class="meta-content fm-sec"><h1 id="_NBK425165_"><span class="title" itemprop="name">Amitriptyline Therapy and <i>CYP2D6</i> and <i>CYP2C19</i> Genotype</span></h1><p class="contrib-group"><span itemprop="author">Laura Dean</span>, MD and <span itemprop="author">Megan Kane</span>, PhD.</p><a data-jig="ncbitoggler" href="#__NBK425165_ai__" style="border:0;text-decoration:none">Author Information and Affiliations</a><div style="display:none" class="ui-widget" id="__NBK425165_ai__"><p class="contrib-group"><h4>Authors</h4><span itemprop="author">Laura Dean</span>, MD<sup>1</sup> and <span itemprop="author">Megan Kane</span>, PhD<sup><img src="/corehtml/pmc/pmcgifs/corrauth.gif" alt="corresponding author" /></sup><sup>2</sup>.</p><h4>Affiliations</h4><div class="affiliation"><sup>1</sup> NCBI<div><span class="email-label">Email: </span><a href="mailto:dev@null" data-email="vog.hin.mln.ibcn@sgm" class="oemail">vog.hin.mln.ibcn@sgm</a></div></div><div class="affiliation"><sup>2</sup> NCBI<div><span class="email-label">Email: </span><a href="mailto:dev@null" data-email="vog.hin.mln.ibcn@sgm" class="oemail">vog.hin.mln.ibcn@sgm</a></div></div><div><sup><img src="/corehtml/pmc/pmcgifs/corrauth.gif" alt="corresponding author" /></sup>Corresponding author.</div></div><p class="small">Created: <span itemprop="datePublished">March 23, 2017</span>; Last Revision: <span itemprop="dateModified">May 18, 2026</span>.</p><p><em>Estimated reading time: 43 minutes</em></p></div><div class="jig-ncbiinpagenav body-content whole_rhythm" data-jigconfig="allHeadingLevels: ['h2'],smoothScroll: false" itemprop="text"><div id="amitriptyline.Introduction" itemprop="description"><h2 id="_amitriptyline_Introduction_">Introduction</h2><p>Amitriptyline is a tricyclic antidepressant used in the treatment of several psychiatric disorders, including major depression, obsessive-compulsive disorder, panic attacks, generalized anxiety disorder, post-traumatic stress disorder, and bulimia. Amitriptyline also has different off-label uses, including migraine prevention, neuropathic pain management, fibromyalgia, and enuresis (bedwetting) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>).</p><p>Tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) primarily mediate their therapeutic effect by inhibiting the reuptake of both serotonin and norepinephrine, leaving more neurotransmitter in the synaptic cleft stimulating the neuron. Given that the tricyclics can also block different receptors (H1 histamine, &#x003b1;1-adrenergic, and muscarinic receptors), side effects are common. As such, selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) have largely replaced the use of TCAs; however, TCAs still have an important use in specific types of depression and other conditions.</p><p>Amitriptyline is metabolized mainly via CYP2C19 and CYP2D6 pathways. Metabolism by CYP2C19 results in active metabolites, including nortriptyline, which is also a tricyclic antidepressant. Metabolism catalyzed by CYP2D6 results in the formation of the less active 10-hydroxy metabolite. Both the <i>CYP2D6</i> and <i>CYP2C19</i> genes are highly polymorphic, resulting in proteins with different enzyme activity or expression levels. Individuals who are CYP2D6 &#x0201c;ultrarapid metabolizers&#x0201d; (UM) have more than 2 normal function alleles (multiple copies), whereas CYP2C19 UM have 2 increased function alleles. Individuals who are CYP2D6 or CYP2C19 &#x0201c;poor metabolizers&#x0201d; (PM) have 2 no function alleles for <i>CYP2D6</i> or <i>CYP2C19</i>.</p><p>The FDA-approved drug label for amitriptyline states that CYP2D6 PM have higher than expected plasma concentrations of tricyclic antidepressants when given usual doses. The FDA recommendations also include monitoring tricyclic antidepressant plasma levels whenever a tricyclic antidepressant is co-administered with another drug known to be an inhibitor of CYP2D6 (<a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobamitriptylineTcyp2d6statusandamitri">Table 1</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>).</p><p>The Dutch Pharmacogenetic Working Group (DPWG) of the Royal Dutch Association for the Advancement of Pharmacy (KNMP) have issued recommendations for altered dosing based on CYP2D6 metabolizer phenotype (<a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobamitriptylineTcypmetabolizerstatusa">Table 2</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.2">2</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.3">3</a>). Individuals who are CYP2D6 UM may require up to 1.6 times the normal dose, though cardiac toxicity risk may indicate use of an antidepressant that is less dependent upon CYP2D6 metabolism, such as citalopram or sertraline. Dose reductions are recommended for CYP2D6 intermediate metabolizer (IM) individuals (75% of normal dose) and CYP2D6 PM individuals (60% of normal dose), along with monitoring for side effects and plasma drug levels. (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.3">3</a>). The DPWG does not recommend any changes in dosing based on CYP2C19 metabolizer status, since the combined dose of amitriptyline and the active metabolite nortriptyline are not significantly impacted. (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.2">2</a>)</p><p>In 2016, the Clinical Pharmacogenetics Implementation Consortium (CPIC) published dosing recommendations for tricyclic antidepressants based on CYP2C19 and CYP2D6 metabolizer status, either individually or in combination (<a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobamitriptylineTcypmetabolizerstatusa1">Table 3</a>, <a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.dosing_recommendations_f/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobamitriptylineTdosingrecommendationsf">Table 4</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.4">4</a>). For CYP2D6 UM, CPIC recommends avoiding the use of a tricyclic due to the potential lack of efficacy, and to consider an alternative drug not metabolized by CYP2D6. If a TCA is still warranted, CPIC recommends considering titrating the TCA to a higher target dose (compared with CYP2D6 normal metabolizers, &#x0201c;NM&#x0201d;) and using therapeutic drug monitoring to guide dose adjustments. For CYP2D6 IM, CPIC recommends considering a 25% reduction of the starting dose, and for CYP2D6 PM, to avoid the use of tricyclics because of the potential for side effects. If a tricyclic is still warranted for CYP2D6 PM, CPIC recommends considering a 50% reduction of the starting dose while monitoring drug plasma concentrations to avoid side effects.</p><p>For CYP2C19 UM, CPIC recommends avoiding the use of tertiary amines (for example, amitriptyline) due to the potential for a sub-optimal response, and to consider an alternative drug not metabolized by CYP2C19, such as the secondary amines nortriptyline or desipramine. For CYP2C19 PMs, CPIC recommends avoiding tertiary amine use due to the potential for sub-optimal response, and to consider an alternative drug not metabolized by CYP2C19. If a tertiary amine is still warranted for CYP2C19 PMs, CPIC recommends considering a 50% reduction of the starting dose while monitoring drug plasma concentrations to avoid side effects (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.4">4</a>). For gene-based dosing of TCAs for neuropathic pain, where the initial doses are lower, CPIC does not recommend dose modifications for PMs or IMs (either CYP2D6 or CYP2C19). For CYP2D6 UM individuals, CPIC optionally recommends considering an alternative medication due to a higher risk of therapeutic failure of TCAs for neuropathic pain (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.4">4</a>).</p></div><div class="h2"></div><div id="amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 1: </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>CYP2D6 Status and Amitriptyline Therapy Management, FDA 2023</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2D6 status</th><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Clinical impact</th><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_3" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Recommended action</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">UM or IM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">May alter systemic concentrations.</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">(None)</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">PM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Higher than expected plasma concentrations when given usual doses</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">(None)</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Any individual with stable TCA therapy with CYP2D6 inhibitor co-medication</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">May become abruptly toxic when given a new CYP2D6 inhibitor as concomitant therapy</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp2d6_status_and_amitri_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">May require lower doses than usually prescribed for either TCA or the other drug; monitor TCA plasma levels whenever a TCA is co-administered with a known CYP2D6 inhibitor</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">UM: Ultrarapid metabolizer; IM: Intermediate metabolizer; PM: Poor metabolizer; TCA: Tricyclic Antidepressant</p></div></dd><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">Table adapted from (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.5">5</a>).</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div><div id="amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 2: </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>CYP Metabolizer Status and Amitriptyline Therapy Recommendations, DPWG 2023</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP status</th><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Clinical impact</th><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_3" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Recommended action</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2D6 UM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">The risk of ineffectiveness is increased and the risk of cardiotoxic side effects may be increased. The gene variation leads to increased conversion of amitriptyline and the active metabolite nortriptyline to less active and inactive metabolites.</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Increase the dose to 1.6 times the normal dose, monitor the effect and side effects or the plasma concentrations and be alert to increased plasma concentrations of the cardiotoxic Z-10-hydroxy metabolites.<br />Plasma concentrations of Z-hydroxy nortriptyline or Z-hydroxy amitriptyline higher than 40 ng/mL are considered toxic.<br />If a dose increase is not desirable due to the cardiotoxic hydroxy metabolite: avoid amitriptyline. <br />Anti-depressants that are not metabolized by CYP2D6, or to a lesser extent, include citalopram and sertraline.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2D6 IM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">The risk of side effects is increased, because the gene variation leads to increased plasma concentrations of the active metabolite nortriptyline and to a lesser extent of amitriptyline.</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Use 75% of the normal dose and monitor the efficacy and side effects or the plasma concentrations of amitriptyline and nortriptyline to adjust the maintenance dose..</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2D6 PM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">In theory, risk of side effects is increased, because the genetic variation results in higher plasma concentrations of the active metabolite nortriptyline and to a lesser extent of amitriptyline.</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Use 60% of the normal dose and monitor the effect and side effects or the plasma concentrations of amitriptyline and nortriptyline to adjust the maintenance dose</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2C19 IM or PM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">The gene variation has an effect on the exposure to amitriptyline, but not on the exposure to amitriptyline + the active metabolite nortriptyline, which determines the effect and side effects.</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">No action is required for this gene-drug interaction.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2C19 UM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">The gene variation decreases the exposure to amitriptyline and increases the exposure to the active metabolite nortriptyline, but there is no evidence to indicate that this results in an increase in side effects or a decrease in efficacy.</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">No action is required for this gene-drug interaction.</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">UM: Ultrarapid metabolizer; NM: normal metabolizer; IM: Intermediate metabolizer; PM: Poor metabolizer</p></div></dd><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">Adapted from (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.2">2</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.3">3</a>)</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div><div id="amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 3: </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>CYP Metabolizer Status and Tricyclic Antidepressant Therapy Recommendations, CPIC 2016</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP status<sup>a</sup></th><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Clinical impact</th><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_3" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Recommended action</th><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_4" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Classification of recommendation for amitriptyline</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2D6 UM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Increased metabolism of TCAs to less active compounds compared with NM.<br />Lower plasma concentrations of active drug will increase probability of pharmacotherapy failure</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Avoid TCA or consider an alternate drug that is not metabolized by CYP2D6. If no alternate medication available, titrate to higher dose and use TDM to guide dosing.</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Strong</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2D6 NM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Normal metabolism of TCAs</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Initiate therapy with recommended starting dose</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Strong</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2D6 IM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="2" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Reduced metabolism of TCAs to less active compounds compared with NM.<br />Higher plasma concentrations of active drug will increase the probability of side effects</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Consider a 25% reduction of recommended starting dose. Utilize TDM to guide dose adjustments.</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Moderate</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2D6 AS = 1</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_3" colspan="1" rowspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Consider a 25% reduction of recommended starting dose. Utilize TDM to guide dose adjustments.</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Optional <sup>b</sup></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2D6 PM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Greatly reduced metabolism of TCAs to less active compounds compared with NM. Higher plasma concentrations of active drug will increase the probability of side effects</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Avoid TCA use due to potential for side effects. Consider alternative drug not metabolized by CYP2D6. If a TCA is warranted, consider a 50% reduction of recommended starting dose. Utilize TDM to guide dose adjustments</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Strong</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2C19 UM/RM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Increased metabolism of tertiary amines compared with normal metabolizers Greater conversion of tertiary amines to secondary amines may affect response or side effects</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Avoid tertiary amine use due to potential for sub-optimal response. Consider alternative drug not metabolized by CYP2C19. TCAs without major CYP2C19 metabolism include the secondary amines nortriptyline and desipramine. If a tertiary amine is warranted, utilize TDM to guide dose adjustments</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Optional</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2C19 NM/IM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Normal or reduced metabolism of tertiary amines.</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Initiate therapy with recommended starting dose.</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Strong</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2C19 PM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Greatly reduced metabolism of tertiary amines compared with normal metabolizers</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Avoid tertiary amine use due to potential for sub-optimal response. Consider alternative drug not metabolized by CYP2C19. TCAs without major CYP2C19 metabolism include the secondary amines nortriptyline and desipramine. For tertiary amines, consider a 50% reduction of the recommended starting dose. Utilize TDM to guide dose adjustments</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.cyp_metabolizer_status_a_1_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Optional</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">UM: Ultrarapid metabolizer; RM: Rapid metabolizer; NM: normal metabolizer; IM: Intermediate metabolizer; PM: Poor metabolizer; TCA: tricyclic antidepressant; TDM: therapeutic drug monitoring</p></div></dd><dt>
<sup>a</sup>
</dt><dd><div id="amitriptyline.TF.3.1"><p class="no_margin">There is limited evidence to guide dosage for non-normal metabolizer status for both CYP2D6 and CYP2C19 in the same individual; TDM is recommended to guide dosing for these individuals.</p></div></dd><dt><sup>b</sup>
</dt><dd><div id="amitriptyline.TF.3.2"><p class="no_margin">This recommendation from a 2019 Update by CPIC (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.6">6</a>).</p></div></dd><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">Table adapted from (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.4">4</a>).</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div><div id="amitriptyline.T.dosing_recommendations_f" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 4: </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Dosing Recommendations for TCA with both CYP2D6 and CYP2C19 Phenotypes (CPIC, 2016)</p></div><p If amitriptyline is warranted, consider a 50% reduction of recommended starting dose <sup>1</sup></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.dosing_recommendations_f_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2C19 PM</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.dosing_recommendations_f_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Avoid amitriptyline use <sup>1</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.dosing_recommendations_f_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Avoid amitriptyline use. If amitriptyline is warranted, consider a 50% reduction of recommended starting dose <sup>3</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.dosing_recommendations_f_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Avoid amitriptyline use <sup>1</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.dosing_recommendations_f_1_1_1_5" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Avoid amitriptyline use <sup>1</sup></td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">UM: Ultrarapid metabolizer; NM: normal metabolizer; IM: Intermediate metabolizer; PM: Poor metabolizer; TCA: tricyclic antidepressant; CPIC: Clinical Pharmacogenetics Implementation Consortium</p></div></dd><dt><sup>1</sup>
</dt><dd><div id="amitriptyline.TF.4.1"><p class="no_margin">Strength of recommendation: optional</p></div></dd><dt><sup>2</sup>
</dt><dd><div id="amitriptyline.TF.4.2"><p class="no_margin">Strength of recommendation: strong</p></div></dd><dt><sup>3</sup>
</dt><dd><div id="amitriptyline.TF.4.3"><p class="no_margin">Strength of recommendation: moderate</p></div></dd><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">Table adapted from (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.4">4</a>).</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div><div id="amitriptyline.Drug_Class_Tricyclic_Antid"><h2 id="_amitriptyline_Drug_Class_Tricyclic_Antid_">Drug Class: Tricyclic Antidepressants</h2><p>Tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) are mixed serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors. They increase the amount of neurotransmitter in the synaptic cleft, a mechanism thought to mediate their antidepressant effects.</p><p>From the 1960s to the 1980s, tricyclics were the first-line treatment for depression, until the introduction of SSRIs, which have fewer side effects and are considered more safe. The common side effects of tricyclics include anticholinergic side effects (for example, blurred vision, dry mouth, constipation, and sedation), cardiac effects, and orthostatic hypotension.</p><p>Today, the main therapeutic use of tricyclics is chronic pain management, such as neuropathic pain. However, tricyclics are still used to treat depression (especially treatment-resistant depression) as well as other psychiatric disorders, including obsessive&#x02013;compulsive disorder, panic attacks, generalized anxiety disorder, post-traumatic stress disorder, bulimia nervosa, smoking cessation, and enuresis (bedwetting).</p><p>Tricyclics are named after their chemical structure of 3 central rings and a side chain important for its function and activity. This structure determines whether a drug is classified a tertiary amine (amitriptyline, clomipramine, doxepin, imipramine, and trimipramine) or secondary amine (desipramine and nortriptyline).</p><p>Tertiary amines are generally more potent at blocking serotonin reuptake, whereas secondary amines are more potent at blocking norepinephrine reuptake. Secondary amines are better tolerated and associated with fewer anticholinergic side effects.</p><p>The CYP2C19 enzyme metabolizes tertiary amines into the active secondary amines, such as desipramine (the active metabolite of imipramine) and nortriptyline (the active metabolite of amitriptyline). Both tertiary and secondary amines are metabolized by CYP2D6 into less active metabolites. The effectiveness and tolerability of tricyclics are, therefore, affected by CYP2D6 metabolism and partially by CYP2C19 metabolism.</p></div><div id="amitriptyline.Drug_Amitriptyline"><h2 id="_amitriptyline_Drug_Amitriptyline_">Drug: Amitriptyline</h2><p>Amitriptyline is used to relieve the symptoms of depression, with endogenous depression being more likely to respond to treatment than other depressive states (namely, reactive depression) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.7">7</a>). Off-label uses of amitriptyline include migraine prevention, and the treatment of neuropathic pain, fibromyalgia, irritable bowel syndrome, and enuresis (bedwetting) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.8">8</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.9">9</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.10">10</a>). It is also recommended for preventative management of tension-type headaches (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.11">11</a>) and has been investigated in management of other types of pain and neuropathy as well as insomnia (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.9">9</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.12">12</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.13">13</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.14">14</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.15">15</a>). It should be noted that these off-label uses often utilize lower doses than the primary indication of depression management.</p><p>Amitriptyline blocks the uptake of both serotonin and norepinephrine, but more potently blocks the reuptake of serotonin. Amitriptyline also has strong affinities for histamine (H1), alpha-1 adrenergic, and muscarinic (M1) receptors, which account for its side effects, including sedation, weight gain, blurred vision, dry mouth, and constipation. The intensity of these side effects tends to be greater for amitriptyline compared to other tricyclics (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.16">16</a>). The frequency of side effects was noted to be particularly high with amitriptyline and topiramate as compared to other medications used for migraine prevention (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.17">17</a>).</p><p>Amitriptyline is metabolized by CYP2C19 to the active metabolite, nortriptyline, which is also a tricyclic antidepressant that is approximately twice as potent as other TCAs. In contrast to amitriptyline, nortriptyline blocks the reuptake of norepinephrine more potently than serotonin (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.16">16</a>). Because both the parent drug (amitriptyline) and the CYP2C19 metabolite (nortriptyline) are pharmacologically active compounds, the plasma levels of both drugs should be monitored as &#x0201c;active moiety&#x0201d; concentration (the sum concentration of amitriptyline and nortriptyline) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.18">18</a>). The active moiety plasma levels may correlate with an individual&#x02019;s response to amitriptyline therapy (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.19">19</a>). Both amitriptyline and nortriptyline are metabolized by CYP2D6 to inactive compounds (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.20">20</a>).</p><p>The optimal therapeutic range for amitriptyline has been well-defined (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.21">21</a>). Most individuals display an optimal response to amitriptyline when combined serum levels of amitriptyline and nortriptyline are between 80 and 200 ng/ml. Higher levels are associated with an increased risk of adverse events. At levels greater than 300 ng/ml, cardiac toxicity occurs. This is characterized by ECG changes (widening of QRS), which may lead to potentially fatal ventricular tachycardia. In some individuals, cardiac toxicity may occur at lower concentrations or even when they are within the recommended therapeutic range (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.22">22</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.23">23</a>). Nortriptyline is metabolized by CYP2D6 to hydroxyl metabolites, which have been associated with cardiac toxicity. Safe levels of hydroxyl metabolites have not yet been defined (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.18">18</a>). Amitriptyline and other TCAs also cause decreased cardiac contractility and hypotension (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.24">24</a>) and have been associated with very rare cases of cardiogenic shock and dilated cardiomyopathy (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.25">25</a>). Historically, amitriptyline has been thought to be among the more toxic tricyclics in the context of medication overdose, though some analyses suggest that nearly all TCAs pose the same potential fatality risk in an overdose (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.26">26</a>). In addition to changes in cardiac rhythm, amitriptyline therapy has also been associated with unhealthy lipid profiles, with higher doses corresponding to more severe abnormalities (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.27">27</a>).</p><p>Additional serious side effects of amitriptyline therapy include worsening of depression, emergence of suicidal ideation, or both; pooled analyses indicate that individuals aged 18-25 were more likely to experience suicidality compared with adults 25 and older (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>). Symptoms of anxiety, agitation, panic attacks, insomnia, hostility, impulsivity, akathisia, mania and hypomania have been observed in individuals treated with antidepressants and should be reported immediately to the health care provider (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>). Individuals with angle-closure glaucoma may experience an angle closure attack in response to normal amitriptyline doses because of the anticholinergic effects. Caution and close supervision are recommended when amitriptyline is administered to individuals with a history of seizures (TCAs lower the seizure threshold) or hyperthyroidism (as a secondary effect). (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>)</p><p>There is no evidence from clinical use that elderly individuals experience reduced efficacy with amitriptyline relative to younger adults, though dosing should be initiated at lower end of the normal range&#x02014;50 mg total per day versus the adult recommended initial dosage of 75 mg per day (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>). Individuals in this population are more likely to have decreased hepatic function, concomitant disease, or multiple medications that may alter drug metabolism. These individuals should also be closely monitored for anticholinergic side effects. A meta-analysis also reported that tricyclic use in older adults was not consistently associated with relief from chronic pain of various types and that risks of side effects were greater than the placebo or comparator medication (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.28">28</a>).</p><p>The FDA drug label does not recommend amitriptyline for individuals under 12 years of age (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>). However, one study of amitriptyline for enuresis used doses of 25 mg for children 6-10 years old and 50 mg for children 10-14 years of age (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.29">29</a>) and a Cochrane review reports that tricyclics imipramine and amitriptyline were more effective at controlling pediatric nocturnal enuresis than placebo (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.30">30</a>). However, this review also notes that efficacy is not sustained at the end of tricyclic therapy and there are uncertainties regarding potentially serious adverse effects of tricyclic medications in pediatric individuals (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.30">30</a>).</p><p>Amitriptyline can cross the placenta and case reports have associated maternal amitriptyline use during pregnancy with adverse effects in the child, including limb deformities, developmental delay and CNS effects (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>). Antidepressant use during the first trimester of pregnancy has also been associated with increased risk of preterm birth (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.31">31</a>), exposure during second or third trimester may increase risk of adverse fetal outcomes (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.32">32</a>). Maternal depression (with or without antidepressant therapy) is associated with increased risk for preterm birth and low APGAR scores (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.33">33</a>). Amitriptyline during pregnancy is recommended only if the potential benefit to the mother justifies the potential risk to the child (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>). Evidence-based clinical practice guidelines for prevention, screening, and treatment of peripartum depression strongly recommend the use of antidepressants after careful consideration of the individual risk-benefit ratio and further caution against discontinuation of antidepressant therapy during pregnancy due to the risk of major relapse (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.34">34</a>). The FDA approved label states that there is a potential for serious adverse reactions in nursing infants with maternal amitriptyline use; the recommended actions are either to discontinue breast feeding or amitriptyline while considering the medical needs of the mother (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>). Data indicates that the amount of amitriptyline and nortriptyline secreted in breastmilk represent an infant dosage of only 1.8% the maternal weight-adjusted dose (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.35">35</a>). Rarely, sedation in a neonate has been observed in association with maternal amitriptyline use, though with infants over 2 months of age it is not expected to cause adverse events (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.35">35</a>) . While one safety scoring system (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.36">36</a>) reports amitriptyline use to be possible with caution during breastfeeding, other agents such as nortriptyline, paroxetine or sertraline may be preferred when larger doses are required or the infant is preterm or newborn (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.35">35</a>).</p><p>Individuals who are carriers of certain variants in <i>CYP2D6</i> or <i>CYP2C19,</i> or both may have drug levels that are outside the therapeutic range after treated with standard doses of amitriptyline. As a result, they may have an increased risk of toxicity (if the level of amitriptyline and its active metabolites are too high) or treatment failure (if drug levels are too low).</p></div><div id="amitriptyline.Gene_CYP2D6"><h2 id="_amitriptyline_Gene_CYP2D6_">Gene: <i>CYP2D6</i></h2><p>The cytochrome P450 superfamily (CYP450) is a large and diverse group of enzymes that form the major system for metabolizing lipids, hormones, toxins, and drugs in the liver. The <i>CYP450</i> genes are highly polymorphic and can result in decreased, absent, or increased enzyme activity. One prominent member, CYP2D6, is responsible for the metabolizing many commonly prescribed drugs, including antidepressants, antipsychotics, analgesics, and beta-blockers.</p><p>The <i>CYP2D6</i> gene is highly polymorphic, with over 170 star (*) alleles described and cataloged by the Pharmacogene Variation (PharmVar) Consortium (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.37">37</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.38">38</a>) Each defined allele is associated with either normal, increased, decreased, or absent enzyme function; however, many of the rare alleles remain uncharacterized and have unknown function (<a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobamitriptylineTactivitystatusofselec">Table 5</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.37">37</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.38">38</a>). PharmVar star (*) alleles are defined by the germline variants detected on a single chromosome and therefore represent independent haplotypes.</p><p>The combination of <i>CYP2D6</i> haplotypes (or alleles) that an individual has is used to determine their diplotype (for example, <i>CYP2D6 *4/*4</i>). Based on their impact on enzyme function, each allele is assigned an activity score from 0 to 1, which in turn is then used to assign a phenotype (for example, CYP2D6 PM). However, the activity score system is not standardized across all clinical laboratories or <i>CYP2D6</i> genotyping platforms, leading to variability among results.</p><p>To promote harmonization, the CPIC and DPWG standardized their <i>CYP2D6</i>&#x000a0;genotype-to-phenotype methods in October 2019, creating a consensus activity scoring guideline. The CYP2D6 phenotype is predicted from the diplotype activity score, which is defined by the sum of the allele score values and typically ranges from 0&#x02013;3.0: (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.39">39</a>)</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>An ultrarapid metabolizer (UM) has an activity score greater than 2.25</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>A normal metabolizer phenotype (NM) has an activity score of 1.25&#x02013;2.25</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>An intermediate metabolizer (IM) has an activity score of &#x0003e;0&#x02013;&#x0003c;1.25</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>A poor metabolizer (PM) has an activity score of 0</div></li></ul><div id="amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 5. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Activity Status of Selected <i>CYP2D6</i> Alleles</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl"
