<i>CYP2D6</i> alleles, please See <a href="https://www.pharmvar.org/gene/CYP2D6" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">the Pharmacogene Variation Consortium</a>. Activity scores from (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.39">39</a>)</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div><p>The <i>CYP2D6*1</i> allele is considered the &#x02018;normal&#x02019; wild-type allele, assigned when no variants are detected, and is associated with normal enzyme activity and the NM phenotype. The <i>CYP2D6*2</i>, <i>*27</i>, and *<i>33</i> alleles are also annotated as having normal activity consistent with the *1 allele.</p><p>Other <i>CYP2D6</i> alleles include variants that produce a non-functioning enzyme (for example, <i>*3</i>, <i>*4</i>, and <i>*6</i>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.40">40</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.41">41</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.42">42</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.43">43</a>) or an enzyme with decreased activity (for example, <i>*10</i>, <i>*17</i>, and <i>*41</i>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.37">37</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.44">44</a>) (<a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobamitriptylineTactivitystatusofselec">Table 5</a>). There are significant interethnic differences in the frequency of these alleles. For example, <i>*3</i>, <i>*4</i>, <i>*6,</i> and <i>*41</i> are more common in individuals of European ancestry, <i>*17</i> is more common in African ancestry, and <i>*10</i> is more common in Asian ancestry. (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.45">45</a>)</p><p>Larger structural variants at the <i>CYP2D6</i> locus have also been described, including gene duplications, deletions, tandem alleles, gene hybrids (namely, <i>CYP2D6</i>-<i>CYP2D7</i>), and gene conversions. As expected, deletions result in a no-function allele (for example, the <i>*5</i> allele is a deletion). Duplications have been reported for alleles with normal function and decreased function. In cases of allele duplications, the activity scores for the full complement of <i>CYP2D6</i> alleles are summed to determine the predicted metabolizer phenotype. Additional details on structural variants are available from PharmVar (see the document Structural Variation for <i>CYP2D6</i>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.46">46</a>).</p><p>The <i>CYP2D6*2, *3,</i>
<i>*4</i>, <i>*5</i>, <i>*6</i>, <i>*9</i>, <i>*10</i>, <i>*17</i>, <i>*29, *41</i> alleles and gene duplications or multiplications are the &#x02018;Tier 1&#x02019; variants recommended by the Association for Molecular Pathology (AMP) for inclusion in <i>CYP2D6</i> clinical genotyping assays .The AMP further recommends that testing laboratories consider <i>*7, *8, *12, *14, *15, *21, *31,</i> *<i>40</i>, *<i>42</i>, *<i>49</i>, *<i>56</i>, *<i>59</i> alleles and <i>CYP2D6-CYP2D7</i> hybrid genes as optional &#x02018;Tier 2&#x02019; variants. These variants have all been shown to have decreased or no function but have either a low minor allele frequency or are technically more challenging to characterize (namely, the hybrid alleles). (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.47">47</a>)</p><p>The frequency of the <i>CYP2D6</i> star (*) alleles with altered function varies across global populations, resulting in different frequencies of the resulting metabolizer phenotype. Given CYP2D6&#x02019;s role in the metabolism of many drugs, the literature on allele and phenotype frequency is expansive. Most populations have a high frequency of normal-function star alleles, leading to a high proportion of the NM individuals. However, reduced-function alleles like <i>CYP2D6*10</i> are highly prevalent in East Asian populations, leading to a higher proportion of IM phenotype individuals in this ancestral group. Many groups in sub-Saharan Africa have higher frequencies of decreased-function alleles like <i>CYP2D6*17</i> and <i>*29</i>, which can correlate with lower metabolizer scores in these individuals. The frequency of UM is notably high in northern Africa, particularly in Algeria, mestizo Columbian population, the Druze ancestry group from the Middle East, and non-Austronesian Melanesian populations (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.48">48</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.49">49</a>). Given the distribution of duplicated, functional <i>CYP2D6</i> alleles, some have proposed that a gene duplication event arose in populations based in the region of Saudi Arabia-Ethiopia, then spread through migration along the Mediterranean, resulting in a higher frequency of UM phenotype in the ancestral populations from these regions (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.50">50</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.51">51</a>). More information on allele and phenotype frequencies is available in the <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/cyp2d6/"><i>CYP2D6</i></a> supplemental chapter.</p><p>
<b>Pharmacologic Conversion of <i>CYP2D6</i> Phenotype</b>
</p><p>Other clinical factors and variables can influence CYP2D6 enzyme activity and, consequently, the metabolizer phenotype of any individual. The administration of an interacting medication can lead to a phenomenon called phenoconversion, wherein an individual with one metabolizer genotype exhibits the enzymatic activity of a different metabolizer group (higher or lower, depending on the medications).</p><p>The enzymatic activity of CYP2D6 can be inhibited or reduced by medications, including strong inhibitors such as paroxetine, fluoxetine, bupropion, and quinidine, as well as moderate inhibitors like duloxetine (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.52">52</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.53">53</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.54">54</a>). This can potentially result in NMs or IMs to respond to medications as if they were PMs, depending on the strength of the enzyme inhibition. Strong inhibitors can completely inhibit CYP2D6 activity, while moderate inhibitors can reduce activity by 50%. Consequently, co-medication with multiple CYP2D6 strong or moderate inhibitors may result in reduced metabolism of drug substrates, as has been observed in psychiatric pharmacotherapy (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.55">55</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.56">56</a>). In contrast, discontinuing a concomitant CYP2D6 inhibitor can restore the individual's CYP2D6 activity to their genetically predicted phenotype baseline. Integration of CYP2D6 phenoconversion into clinical practice is highly relevant (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.57">57</a>), but requires relevant professional expertise to incorporate all factors into clinical decision making. As such, EHR informatic tools have been developed to support clinicians in this process (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.58">58</a>)</p></div><div id="amitriptyline.Gene_CYP2C19"><h2 id="_amitriptyline_Gene_CYP2C19_">Gene: <i>CYP2C19</i></h2><p>The CYP2C19 enzyme contributes to the metabolism of a range of clinically important drugs, including antidepressants, benzodiazepines, voriconazole, some proton pump inhibitors, and the antiplatelet agent, clopidogrel (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.59">59</a>).</p><p>The <i>CYP2C19</i> gene is highly polymorphic, with over 35 variant star (*) alleles cataloged by the <a href="https://www.pharmvar.org/gene/CYP2C19" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">PharmVar</a> Consortium. The <i>CYP2C19*1</i> is considered the normal allele and is categorized with normal enzyme activity and the NM phenotype. Notably, the <i>CYP2C19*1</i> haplotype has been determined to include a single nucleotide polymorphism in the coding region (rs3758581); however, the frequency of the variant nucleotide (G) is nearly 94% globally, and this missense variant does not alter protein function (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.60">60</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.61">61</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.62">62</a>). The <i>CYP2C19*38</i> is defined by the reference sequence with no variants and is also classified as normal function (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.62">62</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.63">63</a>).</p><p>The <i>CYP2C19*17</i> allele is associated with increased enzyme activity and, depending on the number of alleles present, is associated with the &#x0201c;rapid&#x0201d; (RM) (one <i>*17</i> allele) or &#x0201c;ultrarapid&#x0201d; (UM) (<i>*17</i>/<i>*17</i> diplotype) metabolizer phenotype. Non-functional alleles include <i>CYP2C19*2</i> and <i>*3</i>. The <i>CYP2C19</i> IMs have one copy of an allele that encodes a non-functional enzyme (for example, <i>*1/*2</i>), whereas PMs have 2 non-functional alleles (for example, <i>*2/*2</i>, <i>*2/*3</i>) (<a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobamitriptylineTactivitystatusofselec1">Table 6</a>). Individuals with one normal function allele and one decreased function allele are classified as having a &#x0201c;likely IM&#x0201d; phenotype (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.64">64</a>).</p><div id="amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 6. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Activity Status of Selected <i>CYP2C19</i> Alleles</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Allele type</th><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Alleles</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Increased function</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<i>CYP2C19*17</i>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Normal function</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<i>CYP2C19*1</i>
<br />
<i>CYP2C19*38</i>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Decreased function^</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<i>CYP2C19*9</i>
<br />
<i>CYP2C19*10</i>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">No function</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<i>CYP2C19*2</i>
<br />
<i>CYP2C19*3</i>
<br />
<i>CYP2C19*4</i>
<br />
<i>CYP2C19*5</i>
<br />
<i>CYP2C19*6</i>
<br />
<i>CYP2C19*7</i>
<br />
<i>CYP2C19*8</i>
<br />
<i>CYP2C19*35</i>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Uncertain function</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.activity_status_of_selec_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<i>CYP2C19*12</i>
</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">This table is adapted from (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.65">65</a>).</p></div></dd><dt>^</dt><dd><div id="amitriptyline.TF.6.1"><p class="no_margin">Note: the evidence supporting the activity status of decreased-function alleles is limited.</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div><p>Approximately 2% of Caucasians, 4% of African Americans, 14% of Chinese, and 57% of Oceanians are <i>CYP2C19</i> PM, while up to 45% of individuals are <i>CYP2C19</i> IM (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.66">66</a>).</p><p>The most common no-function variant is <i>CYP2C19*2</i>, which is defined by 3 single nucleotide variants: <a href="/nuccore/1464923610" class="bk_tag" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=nuccore">NM_000769.4</a>:c.332-23A&#x0003e;G, <a href="/nuccore/4503218" class="bk_tag" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=nuccore">NM_000769.1</a>:c.681G&#x0003e;A, and <a href="/nuccore/1464923610" class="bk_tag" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=nuccore">NM_000769.4</a>:c.991A&#x0003e;G. These variants result in aberrant mRNA splicing and produce a truncated, non-functioning protein. The <i>CYP2C19*2</i> allele frequencies are between 12&#x02013;18% in individuals of European, American, or African ancestry; 25&#x02013;35% in Asians, Native Hawaiians, and Pacific Islanders; and up to 60% in Oceanian populations (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.66">66</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.67">67</a>).</p><p>For <i>CYP2C19</i>, another commonly tested no-function variant is <i>CYP2C19*3</i>, which contains a c.636G&#x0003e;A variant in exon 4 that causes a premature stop codon. The <i>CYP2C19*3</i> allele frequencies are ~2&#x02013;9% in Asian populations, but are rare in other ancestral populations (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.67">67</a>). Other non-functional variants, such as <i>CYP2C19*4</i>&#x02013;<i>*8,</i> occur in less than 1% of the general population (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.68">68</a>).</p><p>The frequency of the <i>CYP2C19*17</i> allele is approximately 22% in individuals of European ancestry, 8% in individuals from the Americas, 0.5&#x02013;5.7% in Asian, Native Hawaiian, and Pacific Islander populations, 17% in African populations, and 20% in African American and Afro-Caribbean populations (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.66">66</a> , <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.67">67</a>).</p><p>The <i>CYP2C19*2, *3,</i> and <i>*17</i> alleles are the &#x02018;Tier 1&#x02019; alleles recommended by the Association for Molecular Pathology (AMP) for inclusion in <i>CYP2C19</i> clinical genotyping assays (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.69">69</a>). The AMP further recommends that testing laboratories consider <i>*4, *5, *6, *7, *8, *9, *10,</i> and <i>*35</i> alleles as optional &#x02018;Tier 2&#x02019; alleles. These alleles have all been shown to have decreased or no function but have either a low minor allele frequency, limited data characterizing the impact on enzyme function, or lack reference materials. Among the &#x02018;Tier 2&#x02019; alleles, the <i>CYP2C19*35</i> allele is the most common, with a frequency of 9% in African populations (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.69">69</a>).</p><p>
<b>Pharmacologic Conversion of CYP2C19 Phenotype</b>
</p><p>Many medicines are metabolized by the CYP2C19 enzyme, and the enzyme&#x02019;s activity level can be altered by the administration of medications or supplements. Significant changes in effective enzyme activity due to co-medication or non-genetic factors is called phenoconversion. Increased enzymatic activity can result from the induction of CYP2C19, for example, this effect can occur with medications like rifampin, phenytoin, efavirenz, or apalutamide. (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.70">70</a>) St. John&#x02019;s wort and smoking may also increase CYP enzyme activity (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.71">71</a>). Inhibitors of CYP2C19, such as such as omeprazole, fluoxetine, fluvoxamine, fluconazole, or voriconazole, may also impact the target drug metabolism of the target drug (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.70">70</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.72">72</a>). Cannabidiol (CBD) can also inhibit CYP2C19 and increase plasma concentrations of other CYP2C19 substrates (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.73">73</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.74">74</a>). It should be noted that CBD is available in both prescription form and unregulated, over-the-counter supplements, which may lead to unanticipated phenoconversion (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.75">75</a>). The frequency of phenoconversion for CYP2C19 was observed to be much lower than CYP2D6 in a population being treated for psychiatric conditions (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.76">76</a>).</p><p>
<b>Other Genes of Interest: <i>SLC22A1</i></b>
</p><p>The organic cation transporter 1 (OCT1) protein is encoded by <i>SLC22A1</i>. This transport protein has a broad substrate profile and is expressed in hepatocytes and has been investigated as a potential contributor to pharmacokinetics of various medications. (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.77">77</a>) Amitriptyline was observed to cause in vitro inhibition of OCT1 and while healthy study participants showed no change in pharmacokinetics in a single dose study, there was a correlation between reduced OCT1 activity levels and increased plasma amitriptyline in a cohort being treated for depression (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.78">78</a>).</p></div><div id="amitriptyline.Linking_Gene_Variation_wit"><h2 id="_amitriptyline_Linking_Gene_Variation_wit_">Linking Gene Variation with Treatment Response</h2><p>Genetic variation in <i>CYP2D6</i> and <i>CYP2C19</i> have been associated with altered drug levels of amitriptyline among adult individuals (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.79">79</a>).</p><p>
<b>
<i>CYP2D6</i>
</b>
</p><p>Individuals that are CYP2D6 IM have a slightly higher exposure than NM (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.79">79</a>). Plasma concentrations of amitriptyline and nortriptyline showed an increase with decreased CYP2D6 activity and a longer plasma half-life with lower amitriptyline clearance in a study of healthy individuals (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.78">78</a>). While <i>CYP2D6</i> UM genotype individuals are rare, there are observations of even further decreased plasma concentration in these individuals compared to other metabolizer groups, though this difference in pharmacokinetics did not correspond to changes in clinical response (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.57">57</a>).</p><p>Another small study from the Faroe Islands found that PMs (by phenotype, confirmed to be <i>CYP2D6*4/*4</i>) had much lower levels of hydroxylated amitriptyline and nortriptyline compared with NM individuals. However, only 1 of 5 PMs had amitriptyline and nortriptyline total plasma concentration above the recommended range, possibly attributed to lower doses that were clinically indicated in this study cohort. There was no indication that PMs were administered lower doses than NMs. Notably, the PM frequency in this geographically isolated population is reported to be 15%. (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.80">80</a>)</p><p>There is evidence supporting phenoconversion of CYP2D6 to IM or PM can impact amitriptyline exposure in clinical use, leading one study&#x02019;s authors to recommend dose reduction for individuals who are likely to be PMs after accounting for phenoconversion (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.57">57</a>).</p><p>
<b>
<i>CYP2C19</i>
</b>
</p><p>Individuals who are CYP2C19 poor metabolizers have a reduced rate of metabolism of amitriptyline compared with normal metabolizers. As a result, standard doses of amitriptyline lead to higher plasma levels of amitriptyline, lower levels of nortriptyline, and may increase the risk of side effects (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.81">81</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.82">82</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.83">83</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.84">84</a>). Individuals with predicted PM phenotype were more likely than NM to experience treatment emergent mania with amitriptyline therapy for bipolar depression (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.85">85</a>).</p><p>Individuals who are CYP2C19 ultrarapid metabolizers may be at an increased risk of treatment failure, metabolite-based adverse effects, or both. One study found that CYP2C19 UM or RM had lower serum concentration (amitriptyline plus nortriptyline) to dose ratios versus NM (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.76">76</a>). Another study reported higher CYP2C19 activity correlated with higher nortriptyline exposure (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.78">78</a>). Being a carrier of the increased activity allele <i>CYP2C19*17</i> is not associated with an increased level of the sum of amitriptyline plus nortriptyline levels, but the ratio is altered and higher levels of nortriptyline have been reported, which may be linked to increased side effects (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.18">18</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.81">81</a>).</p><p>It should be noted that a systematic review failed to find consistent replication of association between specific genes (including <i>CYP2D6, CYP2C19, ABCB1,</i> and <i>BDNF</i> among others) and antidepressant response or tolerability in children and young adults, possibly attributable to the lack of power in the reviewed studies (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.86">86</a>).</p></div><div id="amitriptyline.Genetic_Testing"><h2 id="_amitriptyline_Genetic_Testing_">Genetic Testing</h2><p>Clinical molecular tests are available for <i>CYP2D6</i> and <i>CYP2C19</i> genotyping from both academic and commercial laboratories. In addition, the NIH&#x02019;s Genetic Testing Registry (GTR) provides a list of tests for <a href="/gtr/all/tests/?term=CN176769%5BDISCUI" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">amitriptyline response</a>, and the <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/gtr/all/tests/?term=1565%5Bgeneid%5D" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri"><i>CYP2D6</i></a> and <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/gtr/all/tests/?term=1557%5Bgeneid%5D" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri"><i>CYP2C19</i></a> genes.</p><p>The available&#x000a0;<i>CYP2D6</i>&#x000a0;tests include targeted single-gene tests as well as multi-gene panels. The AMP has recommended variant&#x000a0;<i>CYP2D6</i>&#x000a0;alleles to be included in clinical genotyping assays (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.87">87</a>), including a minimum panel of &#x02018;Tier 1&#x02019; variant alleles <i>(*2, *3, *4, *5, *6, *9, *10, *17, *29, *41</i> and copy number interrogation) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.47">47</a>). Guidance on recommended alleles for clinical testing of <i>CYP2C19</i> is also available from the AMP, with <i>CYP2C19*2, *3</i> and <i>*17</i> as &#x02018;Tier 1&#x02019; alleles (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.69">69</a>).</p><p>Usually, an individual&#x02019;s result is reported as a diplotype, such as <i>CYP2C19 *1/*1</i> or <i>CYP2D6 *1/*1</i> and may also include an interpretation of the individual&#x02019;s predicted metabolizer phenotype (ultrarapid, rapid, normal, intermediate, or poor). It is important to note that copy number testing is critical when interpreting <i>CYP2D6</i> results (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.88">88</a>). When individuals have more than 2 copies of <i>CYP2D6</i>, the allele copies are denoted by an &#x0201c;xN&#x0201d;, where the &#x0201c;N&#x0201d; can either be quantified or unquantified (for example,&#x000a0;<i>CYP2D6*1/*2x2</i> or <i>CYP2D6 *1/*2xN</i>). Some laboratories also use the notation of DUP to indicate an increase in copy number; however, the report may not always specify the number of duplications or the allele duplicated due to technical limitations.</p><p>The test results may include an interpretation of the individual&#x02019;s predicted metabolizer phenotype, which can be confirmed by checking the diplotype and calculating the&#x000a0;<i>CYP2D6</i>&#x000a0;activity score, as described in the &#x0201c;<i>CYP2D6</i>&#x000a0;Alleles&#x0201d; section above. When a test report does not provide a predicted metabolizer phenotype, resources such as <a href="https://www.pharmgkb.org/page/pharmvar" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">PharmVar</a> are available to assist with predicting the functional impact of identified variants.</p><p>Multiple studies have reported successful implementation of pharmacogenetic testing to guide medication selection or dosing in real-world clinical settings (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.89">89</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.90">90</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.91">91</a>). Of particular concern are cases where individuals are prescribed multiple medications for chronic health conditions, where gene-drug or gene-drug-drug interactions may negatively impact the individual&#x02019;s response to medications (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.92">92</a>).</p></div><div id="amitriptyline.The_CYP2D6_and_CYP2C19_Gen"><h2 id="_amitriptyline_The_CYP2D6_and_CYP2C19_Gen_">The <i>CYP2D6</i> and <i>CYP2C19</i> Gene Interactions with Medications Used for Additional Indications</h2><p>The CYP family of enzymes is involved in the metabolism of many substances and CYP2D6 especially has been implicated in altered pharmacologic responses for many compounds. The drugs can be categorized into many different classes:</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Antipsychotics&#x02014;for example, <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/aripiprazole/">aripiprazole</a>, <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/risperidone/">risperidone</a>, <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/thioridazine/">thioridazine</a> and&#x02014;to a lesser extent&#x02014;<a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/clozapine/">clozapine</a> is metabolized by CYP2D6. According to the FDA, aripiprazole dosage should be reduced for PMs and thioridazine is contraindicated for individuals who are known to have reduced CYP2D6 activity due to increased risk of potentially fatal side effects. The UMs may have a decreased plasma concentration of risperidone, per DPWG recommendations.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Cardiovascular diseases&#x02014;for example, <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/metoprolol/">metoprolol</a>, <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/carvedilol/">carvedilol</a>, and <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/propafenone/">propafenone</a> are used to manage arrhythmias or heart failure and changes in CYP2D6 activity can impact the levels of these medications.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors&#x02014;for example <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/atomoxetine/">atomoxetine</a> and <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/venlafaxine/">venlafaxine</a> may have reduced efficacy in UMs at standard doses while PMs are at risk of elevated plasma concentrations for both medications. The Dutch Pharmacogenetics Working Group advises against the use of venlafaxine in CYP2D6 PMs and IMs.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Anticancer medications&#x02014;for example, <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/tamoxifen/">tamoxifen</a> is activated by CYP2D6, and IMs or PMs may have reduced benefit from tamoxifen therapy.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Pain management&#x02014;for example, <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/codeine/">codeine</a> and <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/tramadol/">tramadol</a> are pro-drugs that require activation by CYP2D6 to achieve the desired analgesic effect. <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/oxycodone/">Oxycodone</a> is also metabolized by CYP2D6, but there are no actionable prescribing recommendations for this gene-drug pair.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Antimalarial medications&#x02014;for example, <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/primaquine/">primaquine</a> must be metabolized by CYP2D6 to form the hydroxylated active metabolite that targets the parasite.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Various therapies for genetic disorders&#x02014;for example <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/eliglustat/">eliglustat</a> used in the treatment of Gaucher disease and <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/deutetrabenazine/">deutetrabenazine</a> and <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/valbenazine/">valbenazine</a> used in the treatment of Huntington disease and tardive dyskinesia may require dose adjustments based on genotype.</div></li></ul><p>It is important to note that <i>CYP2D6</i> is the most common biomarker in drug responses for FDA drug labels; the list provided here is by no means exhaustive. Additional information on gene-drug interactions for&#x000a0;<i>CYP2D6</i>&#x000a0;are available from <a href="https://www.clinpgx.org/gene/PA128" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">ClinPGx</a>, <a href="https://www.clinpgx.org/cpic/pairs" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">CPIC</a> and the <a href="https://www.fda.gov/drugs/science-and-research-drugs/table-pharmacogenomic-biomarkers-drug-labeling" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">FDA</a>&#x000a0;(search for &#x0201c;<i>CYP2D6</i>&#x0201d;).</p><p>Other medications affected by&#x000a0;<i>CYP2C19</i>&#x000a0;genetic variation may be used to treat:</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Acute coronary syndrome &#x02013;&#x02013; individuals who are CYP2C19 poor metabolizers and undergoing percutaneous coronary intervention have an increased risk of cardiovascular events if they are treated with the antiplatelet drug <a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/clopidogrel/">clopidogrel</a> (a pro-drug that is activated by CYP2C19-mediated metabolism).</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Gastrointestinal ulcers, gastroesophageal reflux, erosive esophagitis, and&#x000a0;Helicobacter pylori&#x000a0;infection&#x02014;PPIs like&#x000a0;<a href="/sites/books/n/gtrbook/omeprazole/">omeprazole</a>,&#x000a0;<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/n/gtrbook/esomeprazole/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">esomeprazole</a>, and others may have reduced metabolism in CYP2C19 IMs and PMs and thus these individuals have higher exposure to these medications, which imparts risk of adverse events. Conversely,&#x000a0;<i>CYP2C19*17</i>&#x000a0;confers increased activity and thus a more rapid clearance of PPIs and potential for treatment failure in UMs.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Antifungal treatment&#x02014;<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/n/gtrbook/voriconazole/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">voriconazole</a>&#x000a0;is metabolized by CYP2C19 and UMs may have delayed target blood concentrations due to rapid clearance; PMs may experience high exposure and have a risk of adverse events.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Depression, anxiety disorders, obsessive-compulsive disorder, or migraine prophylaxis&#x02014;both selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs, such as citalopram, escitalopram, and sertraline) and tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs, such as the tertiary amines&#x000a0;clomipramine, doxepin,&#x000a0;<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/n/gtrbook/imipramine/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">imipramine</a>, and trimipramine) can be metabolized by <i>CYP2C19</i>. When administering SSRIs, UMs may experience treatment failure due to high clearance while PMs may require dose reduction. For the tertiary amine TCAs, UMs may experience altered responses or side effects due to rapid conversion to secondary amines while PMs are at risk of suboptimal responses and may require a lower dose.&#x000a0;<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/n/gtrbook/diazepam/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">Diazepam</a>&#x000a0;is also partially metabolized by <i>CYP2C19</i> and altered enzyme function may contribute to altered clearance of this medication.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Epilepsy or seizures&#x02014;<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/n/gtrbook/brivaracetam/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">brivaracetam</a>, used for partial-onset (focal) epilepsy, is partially metabolized by <i>CYP2C19</i> and PMs may require lower doses to avoid adverse effects due to reduced clearance of this medication.&#x000a0;<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/n/gtrbook/clobazam/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">Clobazam</a>&#x000a0;is used to manage seizures in a variety of conditions and <i>CYP2C19</i> PMs may experience higher exposure to norclobazam, putting them at higher risk for adverse effects.&#x000a0;<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/n/gtrbook/lacosamide/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">Lacosamide</a>&#x000a0;is also metabolized by <i>CYP2C19</i>, though there is no indication that PMs require altered dosing or management.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Musculoskeletal pain&#x02014;<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/n/gtrbook/carisoprodol/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">carisoprodol</a>, a centrally acting muscle relaxant, is metabolized by <i>CYP2C19</i> and PMs may be at a higher risk of carisoprodol toxicity.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Hypoactive sexual desire disorder&#x02014;<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/n/gtrbook/flibanserin/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">flibanserin</a>&#x000a0;is metabolized, in part, by <i>CYP2C19</i> and PMs have a higher exposure to this medication, resulting in an elevated risk of hypotension, syncope, and CNS depression.</div></li></ul><p>Additional information on gene-drug interactions for&#x000a0;<i>CYP2C19</i>&#x000a0;are available from&#x000a0;<a href="https://www.clinpgx.org/gene/PA124/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">ClinPGx</a>,&#x000a0;<a href="https://www.clinpgx.org/cpic/pairs" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">CPIC</a>&#x000a0;and the&#x000a0;<a href="https://www.fda.gov/drugs/science-and-research-drugs/table-pharmacogenomic-biomarkers-drug-labeling" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">FDA</a>&#x000a0;(search for &#x0201c;<i>CYP2C19</i>&#x0201d;).</p></div><div id="amitriptyline.Therapeutic_Recommendation"><h2 id="_amitriptyline_Therapeutic_Recommendation_">Therapeutic Recommendations Based on Genotype</h2><p><b>This section contains excerpted</b><sup><a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.FN1">1</a></sup>
<b>information on gene-based dosing recommendations. Neither this section nor other parts of this review contain the complete recommendations from the sources.</b></p><div id="amitriptyline.2024_Statement_from_the_US"><h3>2024 Statement from the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA)</h3><p>The biochemical activity of the drug metabolizing isozyme cytochrome P450 2D6 (debrisoquin hydroxylase) is reduced in a subset of the Caucasian population (about 7 to 10% of Caucasians are so called &#x0201c;poor metabolizers&#x0201d;); reliable estimates of the prevalence of reduced P450 2D6 isozyme activity among Asian, African and other populations are not yet available. Poor metabolizers have higher than expected plasma concentrations of tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) when given usual doses. Depending on the fraction of drug metabolized by P450 2D6, the increase in plasma concentration may be small, or quite large (8 fold increase in plasma AUC of the TCA).</p><p>In addition, certain drugs inhibit the activity of this isozyme and make normal metabolizers resemble poor metabolizers. An individual who is stable on a given dose of TCA may become abruptly toxic when given one of these inhibiting drugs as concomitant therapy. The drugs that inhibit cytochrome P450 2D6 include some that are not metabolized by the enzyme (quinidine; cimetidine) and many that are substrates for P450 2D6 (many other antidepressants, phenothiazines, and the Type 1C antiarrhythmics propafenone and flecainide). While all the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs), e.g., fluoxetine, sertraline, and paroxetine, inhibit P450 2D6, they may vary in the extent of inhibition. The extent to which SSRI-TCA interactions may pose clinical problems will depend on the degree of inhibition and the pharmacokinetics of the SSRI involved. Nevertheless, caution is indicated in the coadministration of TCAs with any of the SSRIs and also in switching from one class to the other. Of particular importance, sufficient time must elapse before initiating TCA treatment in a patient being withdrawn from fluoxetine, given the long half-life of the parent and active metabolite (at least 5 weeks may be necessary).</p><p>Concomitant use of tricyclic antidepressants with drugs that can inhibit cytochrome P450 2D6 may require lower doses than usually prescribed for either the tricyclic antidepressant or the other drug. Furthermore, whenever one of these other drugs is withdrawn from co-therapy, an increased dose of tricyclic antidepressant may be required. It is desirable to monitor TCA plasma levels whenever a TCA is going to be coadministered with another drug known to be an inhibitor of P450 2D6.</p><p>
<b>FDA Pharmacogenetics Associations for which the data demonstration a potential impact on pharmacokinetic properties only</b>
</p><div id="amitriptyline.Te" class="table"><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.Te/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__amitriptyline.Te_lrgtbl__"><table><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.Te_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Drug</th><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.Te_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Gene</th><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.Te_1_1_1_3" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Affected Subgroups</th><th id="hd_h_amitriptyline.Te_1_1_1_4" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Description of Gene-Drug interaction</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.Te_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Amitriptyline</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.Te_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">CYP2D6</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.Te_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Ultrarapid, intermediate, or poor metabolizers</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.Te_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">May alter systemic concentrations</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><p><b>Please review the complete therapeutic recommendations that are located here:</b> (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.1">1</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.5">5</a>).</p></div><div id="amitriptyline.2023_Summary_of_recommenda"><h3>2023 Summary of recommendations from the Pharmacogenetics Working Group of the Royal Dutch Association for the Advancement of Pharmacy (KNMP)</h3><p>CYP2D6 UM: amitriptyline</p><p>The risk of ineffectiveness is increased and the risk of cardiotoxic side effects may be increased. The gene variation leads to increased conversion of amitriptyline and the active metabolite nortriptyline to less active and inactive metabolites.</p><p>- increase the dose to 1.6 times the normal dose, monitor the effect and side effects or the plasma concentrations and be alert to increased plasma concentrations of the cardiotoxic Z-10-hydroxy metabolites</p><p>Plasma concentrations of Z-hydroxy nortriptyline or Z-hydroxy amitriptyline higher than 40 ng/mL are considered toxic.</p><p>- if a dose increase is not desirable due to the cardiotoxic hydroxy metabolite: avoid amitriptyline. Anti-depressants that are not metabolised by CYP2D6, or to a lesser extent, include citalopram and sertraline.</p><p>CYP2D6 IM: amitriptyline</p><p>The risk of side effects is increased, because the gene variation leads to higher plasma concentrations of the active metabolite nortriptyline and to a lesser extent of amitriptyline.</p><p>- use 75% of the normal dose and monitor the efficacy and side effects or the plasma concentrations of amitriptyline and nortriptyline to adjust the maintenance dose</p><p>CYP2D6 PM: amitriptyline</p><p>In theory, risk of side effects is increased, because the genetic variation results in higher plasma concentrations of the active metabolite nortriptyline and to a lesser extent of amitriptyline.</p><p>- use 60% of the normal dose and monitor the effect and side effects or the plasma concentrations of amitriptyline and nortriptyline to adjust the maintenance dose</p><p>CYP2C19 IM: amitriptyline</p><p>NO action is required for this gene-drug interaction.</p><p>The gene variation has an effect on the exposure to amitriptyline, but not on the exposure to amitriptyline + the active metabolite nortriptyline, which determines the effect and side effects.</p><p>CYP2C19 PM: amitriptyline</p><p>NO action is required for this gene-drug interaction.</p><p>The gene variation has an effect on the exposure to amitriptyline, but not on the exposure to amitriptyline + the active metabolite nortriptyline, which determines the effect and side effects.</p><p>CYP2C19 UM: amitriptyline</p><p>NO action is required for this gene-drug interaction.</p><p>The gene variation decreases the exposure to amitriptyline and increases the exposure to the active metabolite nortriptyline, but there is no evidence to indicate that this results in an increase in side effects or a decrease in efficacy. A higher dose is required to achieve the therapeutic range of amitriptyline+nortriptyline, but the therapeutic range of nortriptyline is achieved at the lower dose.</p><p><b>Please review the complete therapeutic recommendations and their most recent updates that are located here:</b> (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.2">2</a>).</p></div><div id="amitriptyline.2016_Statement_from_the_Cl"><h3>2016 Statement from the Clinical Pharmacogenetics Implementation Consortium (CPIC)</h3><p>
<i>CYP2D6 dosing recommendations.</i>
</p><p>[&#x02026;]. The recommended starting dose of amitriptyline or nortriptyline does not need adjustment for those with genotypes predictive of CYP2D6 normal metabolism. A 25% reduction of the recommended dose may be considered for CYP2D6 intermediate metabolizers. The strength of this recommendation is classified as &#x0201c;moderate&#x0201d; because patients with a CYP2D6 activity score of 1.0 are inconsistently categorized as intermediate or normal metabolizers in the literature, making these studies difficult to evaluate.</p><p>In the October 2019 update from CPIC: &#x0201c;The recommendation for CYP2D6 IM (reduce starting dose by 25%) should be considered for CYP2D6 AS of 1&#x000a0;(strength of recommendation: optional).&#x0201d;</p><p>CYP2D6 ultrarapid metabolizers have a higher probability of failing amitriptyline or nortriptyline pharmacotherapy due to subtherapeutic plasma concentrations, and alternate agents are preferred. There are documented cases of CYP2D6 ultrarapid metabolizers receiving large doses of nortriptyline in order to achieve therapeutic concentrations. However, very high plasma concentrations of the nortriptyline hydroxy-metabolite were present, which may increase the risk for cardiotoxicity. If a tricyclic is warranted, there are insufficient data in the literature to calculate a starting dose for a patient with CYP2D6 ultrarapid metabolizer status, and therapeutic drug monitoring is strongly recommended.</p><p>Adverse effects are more likely in CYP2D6 poor metabolizers due to elevated tricyclic plasma concentrations; therefore, alternate agents are preferred. If a tricyclic is warranted, consider a 50% reduction of the usual dose, and therapeutic drug monitoring is strongly recommended.</p><p>
<i>CYP2C19 dosing recommendations.</i>
</p><p>[&#x02026;]. The usual starting dose of amitriptyline may be used in CYP2C19 normal and intermediate metabolizers. Although CYP2C19 intermediate metabolizers would be expected to have a modest increase in the ratio of amitriptyline to nortriptyline plasma concentrations, the evidence does not indicate that CYP2C19 intermediate metabolizers should receive an alternate dose.</p><p>Patients taking amitriptyline who are CYP2C19 rapid or ultrarapid metabolizers may be at risk for having low plasma concentrations and an imbalance between parent drug and metabolites causing treatment failure and/or adverse events. Although the CYP2C19*17 allele did not alter the sum of amitriptyline plus nortriptyline plasma concentrations, it was associated with higher nortriptyline plasma concentrations, possibly increasing the risk of adverse events. For patients taking amitriptyline, extrapolated pharmacokinetic data suggest that CYP2C19 rapid or ultrarapid metabolizers may need a dose increase. Due to the need for further studies investigating the clinical importance of CYP2C19*17 regarding tricyclic metabolism and the possibility of altered concentrations, we recommend to consider an alternative tricyclic or other drug not affected by CYP2C19. This recommendation is classified as optional due to limited available data. If amitriptyline is administered to a CYP2C19 rapid or ultrarapid metabolizer, therapeutic drug monitoring is recommended.</p><p>CYP2C19 poor metabolizers are expected to have a greater ratio of amitriptyline to nortriptyline plasma concentrations. The elevated amitriptyline plasma concentrations may increase the chance of a patient experiencing side effects. Use an alternative agent not metabolized by CYP2C19 (e.g., nortriptyline and desipramine) or consider a 50% reduction of the usual amitriptyline starting dose along with therapeutic drug monitoring.</p><p><b>Please review the complete therapeutic recommendations that are located here:</b> (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.4">4</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.6">6</a>).</p></div></div><div id="amitriptyline.Nomenclature"><h2 id="_amitriptyline_Nomenclature_">Nomenclature</h2><div id="amitriptyline.T.nomenclature_for_selecte" class="table"><div class="caption"><p>Nomenclature for selected <i>CYP2D6</i> alleles</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK425165/table/amitriptyline.T.nomenclature_for_selecte/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" scope="rowgroup" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>CYP2D6*2</i>
<sup>[1]</sup>
</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.nomenclature_for_selecte_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">2851C&#x0003e;T</td><td headers="hd_h_amitriptyline.T.nomenclature_for_selecte_1_1_1_3 hd_h_amitriptyline.T.nomenclature_for_selecte_1_1_2_1" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/clinvar/variation/242771/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">NM_000106.6:c.886C&#x0003e;T</a>

</dt><dd><div id="amitriptyline.TF.g.1"><p class="no_margin">AMP Tier 1 allele (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.69">69</a>)</p></div></dd><dt>

</dt><dd><div id="amitriptyline.TF.g.2"><p class="no_margin">AMP Tier 2 allele (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.69">69</a>)</p></div></dd><dt>
<sup>[3]</sup>
</dt><dd><div id="amitriptyline.TF.g.3"><p class="no_margin">The <i>CYP2C19*17</i> allele has increased expression due to an upstream, non-coding variant. The legacy HGVS expression for the change relative to the coding sequence is provided, but the correct RefSeq genomic sequence is provided as well.</p></div></dd><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">Guidelines for the description and nomenclature of gene variations are available from the Human Genome Variation Society (HGVS) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.93">93</a>)</p></div></dd><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">Nomenclature for Cytochrome P450 enzymes is available from the PharmVar database (<a class="bk_pop" href="#amitriptyline.REF.37">37</a>) <a href="https://www.pharmvar.org/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">https://www<wbr style="display:inline-block"></wbr>​.pharmvar.org/</a></p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div><div id="amitriptyline.Acknowledgments"><h2 id="_amitriptyline_Acknowledgments_">Acknowledgments</h2><p>Version 1.0 (Published March 23, 2017)</p><p>The author would like to thank the following individuals for reviewing this summary: David Kisor, B.S., Pharm.D., Professor and Director of Pharmacogenomics Education, Pharmacogenomics Program, Manchester University, Indiana; Mohamed Nagy, Clinical Pharmacist, Head of the Personalised Medication Management Unit, Department of pharmaceutical Services, Children's Cancer Hospital, Egypt; Yolande Saab, Pharm.D., Ph.D., Associate Professor of Pharmacogenomics, School of Pharmacy, Lebanese American University, Lebanon; Stuart Scott, Assistant Professor of Genetics and Genomic Sciences, Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai; Chakradhara Rao S Uppugunduri, Ma&#x000ee;tre-assistant at the CANSEARCH Laboratory, University of Geneva, Switzerland; and Mandy van Rhenen, secretary of the Dutch Pharmacogenetics Working Group (DPWG), Centre for Information on Medicines, Royal Dutch Pharmacists Association (KNMP).</p><p>Version 2.0 (Published June 23, 2025)</p><p>The authors would like to acknowledge Maike Scherf-Clavel, Dr. rer. This chapter includes updated guidance from the Dutch Pharmacogenetics Working Group regarding <i>CYP2D6</i> genotype-guided dosing of amitriptyline.
